     1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
     2	
     3	\section{Introduction}
     4	\label{sec:introduction}
     5	
     6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
     7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
     8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with \emph{constraint tightness} (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
     9	Type violations are ontologically invalid regardless of downstream application: a triple whose head or tail type lies outside the relation's domain/range specification contradicts the schema definition.
    10	At deployment-grade quality (R-F1 40--46\%), even 1--2\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption; type violation elimination ensures ontological consistency of constructed KGs independent of extraction F1, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.
    11	
    12	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
    13	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
    14	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
    15	Post-hoc filtering improves precision but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives; at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral Do

1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	
3	OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a decidability-guided two-layer architecture.
4	LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%, CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC under favorable conditions (1.54$\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\% for calibrated models.
5	Beam search ($k{\geq}4$) substantially reduces CF under low-$\bar{\kappa}$ or fine-tuned conditions; $\bar{\kappa}$ provides a descriptive diagnostic for deployment guidance, though validation on additional schemas is needed.
6	Evaluation covers primarily 7--8B models with preliminary 70B results.
7

_source line 6788 · 2026-05-21T02:56:49.357111+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes.
4	The two layers exhibit a formal enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning, \textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR (94--100\% reduction) with negligible R-F1 impact ($\pm$0.6\,pp) but introduces cross-field (CF) violations as a trade-off (0--9.9\% zero-shot, $\leq$1.2\% fine-tuned; \S\ref{sec:scierc-results}).
7	CF is substantially reduced by beam search under favorable conditions (low $\bar{\kappa}$ or fine-tuned settings).
8	The framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across four autoregressive LMs (7--70B; 70B evaluated in INT4 only, \S\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).
9	\end{abstract}
10

1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	Type violations are ontologically invalid regardless of downstream application: a triple whose head or tail type lies outside the relation's domain/range specification contradicts the schema definition.
10	At deployment-grade quality (R-F1 40--46\%), even 1--2\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption; type violation elimination ensures ontological consistency of constructed KGs independent of extraction F1, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.
11	
12	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
13	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
14	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
15	Post-hoc filtering improves precision but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives; at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\%), it becomes impractical.

23	
24	\smallskip
25	\noindent\textbf{Proposition 1} (Level 2 $\not\subseteq$ Level 1)\textbf{.}
26	\textit{Domain/range type constraints for IE with unbounded entity sets are not context-free.}
27	
28	\smallskip
29	\noindent\textit{Proof sketch.}
30	Consider $\mathcal{E} = \{\tau_A, \tau_B\}$ and a relation $r$ with $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau_A\}$.
31	An output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types ($a_i \in \{A, B\}$), followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \leq n$.
32	Validating the head-type field requires retrieving $\tau_{a_k}$, the type assigned at position $k$ in the entity list.
33	A PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k < n$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint for all valid outputs.
34	OntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups), operating as a deterministic stateful transducer over the token stream.
35	See Appendix~\ref{app:pumping-lemma} for a full proof via the pumping lemma.\hfill$\square$
36	
37	\noindent\textbf{Corollary} (enabling dependency: a constraint whose satisfaction is a prerequisite for evaluating subsequent constraints)\textbf{.} The enabling dependency is necessary: the grammar layer alone cannot enforce domain/range constraints (Proposition~1), while the semantic layer alone achieves only 24\% DR reduction without grammar-enforced vocabulary membership (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}, Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}).
38	\smallskip
39	
40	We describe the problem formulation (\S\ref{sec:formulation}), each layer, and two decoding strategies (\S\ref{sec:strategies}); formal analysis of constraint composition is in Section~\ref{sec:composition}.
41	
42

1	% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)
2	
3	At each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \subseteq V$ and $S(t) \subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.
4	The final mask is their conjunction:
5	%
6	\begin{equation}\label{eq:composition}
7	  M_{\text{final}}(t) = M_{\text{grammar}}(t) \;\wedge\; M_{\text{semantic}}(t),
8	\end{equation}
9	%
10	where $\wedge$ denotes element-wise AND over the vocabulary $V$; a token is allowed only if it satisfies \emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.
11	The conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$. This reflects the enabling dependency (\S\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\mathcal{R}$, a property enforced only by the grammar layer.
12	
13	\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}
14	When $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\"{\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.
15	We enforce a \emph{grammar-first fallback}: semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.
16	Structural integrity is a hard constraint; type violations are soft errors detectable by downstream validation, with DR (ontologically invalid type pair) more severe than CF (individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency).
17	
18	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}
19	For relation $r$:
20	%
21	\begin{equation}\label{eq:tightness}
22	  \kappa(r) = 1 - \frac{|\sigma(r)|}{|\mathcal{E}|^2},
23	\end{equation}
24	%
25	The mean $\bar{\kappa} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}\sum_{r} \kappa(r)$ characterizes schema restrictiveness: SciERC ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) achieves 94--99\% DR reduction vs.\ DocRED ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$) at 42--90\%; entity type confusability is a critical co-factor (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa-case-study},~\ref{app:tcr}).
26

44	\label{sec:formulation}
45	
46	\paragraph{IE as structured generation.}
47	Given an input text $x$, a language model generates a structured JSON output $y$:
48	%
49	\begin{equation}\label{eq:output-schema}
50	\resizebox{.88\columnwidth}{!}{$\displaystyle
51	  y = \bigl\{\texttt{entities}: [\{n_i, \tau_i\}],\; \texttt{relations}: [\{h_j, t_j, r_j, \tau^h_j, \tau^t_j\}]\bigr\}
52	$}
53	\end{equation}
54	%
55	where each entity has a name $n_i$ and type $\tau_i \in \mathcal{E}$, and each relation specifies head $h_j$, tail $t_j$, relation type $r_j \in \mathcal{R}$, and head/tail entity types $\tau^h_j, \tau^t_j$.
56	The output JSON Schema (\S\ref{sec:grammar}) enforces a fixed field ordering (head $\to$ tail $\to$ relation $\to$ head\_type $\to$ tail\_type) via deterministic grammar compilation, ensuring the relation type $r_j$ is known before entity type fields and enabling sequential domain/range lookups (\S\ref{sec:dynamic-masking}).
57	The grammar structurally enforces entity-before-relation ordering (100\% preservation across all configurations), ensuring the entity registry (\S\ref{sec:entity-registry}) is populated before relation-level type constraints are evaluated; unregistered entities trigger domain-only filtering.
58	
59	\paragraph{Ontology compliance.}
60	Using the ontology $\mathcal{O} = (\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{R}, \sigma)$ defined in \S\ref{sec:background}, we write $\operatorname{dom}(r) = \{\tau^h : \exists \tau^t,\, (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for domain-valid head types and $\operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h) = \{\tau^t : (\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)\}$ for the conditional range given head type $\tau^h$.
61	Our enforcement target is \emph{pair-based} type validity: $(\tau^h, \tau^t) \in \sigma(r)$ for every triple.
62	The decoder enforces this sequentially: $\tau^h \in \operatorname{dom}(r)$ at head position, then $\tau^t \in \operatorname{range}(r, \tau^h)$ at tail position, achieving best-effort pair-based enforcement (pair validity ensured

5	\label{sec:violation_landscape}
6	
7	We evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 (\S\ref{sec:composability}), near-eliminating DR (DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%) with minimal overhead; and LoRA + beam S1 (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}), achieving zero total violations on SciERC under favorable conditions (1.54$\times$ latency).
8	Zero-shot evaluation (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}--\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) serves as a diagnostic probe to isolate constraint effects; R-F1 2--8 reflects absent task-specific training, not method failure.
9	The deployment-relevant evaluation is the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 40--46\%), where constraints compose with parameter optimization without degrading performance.
10	
11	\paragraph{Setup.}
12	We evaluate on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$).
13	RE type constraints are extracted automatically from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) are derived from the KAIROS ontology.
14	The framework handles both sources without modification.
15	Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \S\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \S\ref{sec:composability}.
16	We measure four violation categories: \textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\notin \sigma(r)$), \textbf{RV} (relation $\notin \mathcal{R}$), \textbf{ET} (entity type $\notin \mathcal{E}$), and \textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).

_source line 6824 · 2026-05-21T02:59:35.390808+00:00_

1	% Section 2: Background (compressed)
2	
3	\section{Background}
4	\label{sec:background}
5	
6	\paragraph{IE Ontology Structure.}
7	\label{sec:bg:ontology}
8	An IE ontology $\mathcal{O} = (\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{R}, \sigma)$ defines entity types $\mathcal{E}$, relation types $\mathcal{R}$, and a mapping $\sigma: \mathcal{R} \to \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{E} \times \mathcal{E})$, where $\mathcal{P}(\cdot)$ denotes the power set, specifying valid (head type, tail type) pairs per relation.
9	A triple $(h, r, t)$ is \emph{type-valid} if $(\mathrm{type}(h), \mathrm{type}(t)) \in \sigma(r)$.
10	We quantify constraint restrictiveness via \emph{constraint tightness} $\kappa(r)$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:tightness}, \S\ref{sec:composition}): tighter constraints leave fewer valid type pairs per relation.
11	We define ontology constraints broadly to encompass both formal ontology specifications (e.g., the KAIROS event ontology with explicit role-entity type mappings) and empirically derived type-pair constraints extracted from annotated training data.
12	The latter applies when datasets lack a formal ontology but provide typed annotations (SciERC, DocRED); OntoCoDec compiles both sources into the same constraint representation without modification.
13	Empirical constraints achieve 99\%+ instance-level coverage on held-out data (SciERC 99.0\%, DocRED 99.4\%); uncovered type pairs trigger grammar-first fallback (\S\ref{sec:composition}), ensuring no valid triples are blocked.
14	
15	\paragraph{Grammar-Constrained Decoding.}

62:Full S1 reduces DR from 18.46\% to 10.21\% ($\downarrow$45\%), eliminates ET ($\to$\,0\%), and eliminates RV (29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).\footnote{The residual 0.03\% RV traces to a rare XGrammar automaton edge case where subword tokens from different enum entries combine into chimeric values (e.g., \texttt{member} + \texttt{icipant\_of} from \texttt{member\_of} and \texttt{participant\_of}). This is not a fundamental limitation of the approach.}

  "old_string": "\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textit{Proof sketch.}\nConsider $\\mathcal{E} = \\{\\tau_A, \\tau_B\\}$ and a relation $r$ with $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau_A\\}$.\nAn output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types ($a_i \\in \\{A, B\\}$), followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \\leq n$.\nValidating the head-type field requires retrieving $\\tau_{a_k}$, the type assigned at position $k$ in the entity list.\nA PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k < n$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint for all valid outputs.\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups), operating as a deterministic stateful transducer over the token stream.\nFor finite entity sets, the valid language is technically regular but requires an exponentially large DFA ($>$10$^{17}$ states for SciERC), making PDA-based approaches practically necessary.\nSee Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma} for a full proof via the pumping lemma.\\hfill$\\square$",
  "new_string": "\\smallskip\n\\noindent\\textit{Proof sketch.}\nConsider $\\mathcal{E} = \\{\\tau_A, \\tau_B\\}$ and a relation $r$ with $\\operatorname{dom}(r) = \\{\\tau_A\\}$.\nAn output contains $n$ entities with independently chosen types, followed by a relation referencing head $e_k$ for arbitrary $k \\leq n$.\nA PDA's LIFO stack cannot retrieve position $k < n$ without destroying entries above it; since both $n$ and $k$ are independently unbounded, no PDA can enforce this constraint.\nOntoCoDec's semantic layer addresses this with a runtime entity registry $\\mathcal{D}$ (a finite map enabling random-access type lookups).\nFor finite entity sets, the valid language is technically regular but requires an exponentially large DFA ($>$10$^{17}$ states for SciERC), making PDA-based approaches practically necessary (Appendix~\\ref{app:pumping-lemma}).\\hfill$\\square$"
}

_source line 6884 · 2026-05-21T03:25:21.735215+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}2$ schemas).
4	The two layers exhibit an enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning, \textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR (92--100\% reduction) with negligible R-F1 impact ($\pm$0.6\,pp) but introduces cross-field (CF) violations as a trade-off (0--21.1\% zero-shot, $\leq$1.3\% fine-tuned, dataset-dependent; \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
7	CF is substantially reduced by beam search under favorable conditions (low $\bar{\kappa}$ or fine-tuned settings).
8	The framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across four autoregressive LMs (7--70B; 70B as feasibility check in INT4 only, \S\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).
9	\end{abstract}
10

_source line 6890 · 2026-05-21T03:25:40.863966+00:00_

17	
18	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}
19	\label{eq:tightness}
20	We define per-relation constraint tightness $\kappa(r) = 1 - |\sigma(r)|/|\mathcal{E}|^2$ and the schema-level mean $\bar{\kappa} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}\sum_{r} \kappa(r)$.
21	SciERC ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) achieves 94--99\% DR reduction vs.\ DocRED ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$) at 42--90\%; entity type confusability is a critical co-factor (Appendix~\ref{app:kappa-case-study},~\ref{app:tcr}).
22	
23	\paragraph{Greedy limitation and fallback cascade.}
24	\label{sec:greedy-limitation}
25	The semantic layer commits entity types greedily---an instance of Level~3 (non-local) constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability})---so CF violations arise when the same entity receives different types across triples; beam search substantially reduces this (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
26	At semantically meaningful positions (first token of each type field), grammar-first fallback triggers on 0.5--5.7\% of positions under zero-shot decoding, correlating with $\bar{\kappa}$ (SciERC $\leq$\,0.5\%; DocRED 1.4--5.7\%); the higher token-level rate (36.6\%; Appendix~\ref{app:fallback-coverage}) reflects multi-token type name continuations.

15	% exp_ids: 70b_scierc_unconstrained, 70b_scierc_constrained, 70b_docred_unconstrained, 70b_docred_constrained
16	\paragraph{Preliminary 70B evaluation.}
17	\label{sec:70b-results}
18	On Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), OntoCoDec achieves DR$\to$0\% on both SciERC (R-F1 $-$0.03\,pp) and DocRED (R-F1 $+$0.04\,pp), one of three configurations to eliminate DR on DocRED.
19	CF rises to 7.01\% (from 0.07\%), partly because constrained decoding exposes more triples to the consistency check (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
20	
21	\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
22	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}
23	Our grammar-only ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same class of constraints---vocabulary membership via context-free grammars---as grammar-constrained decoding systems such as GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and the grammar component of BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}.
24	This ablation achieves RV/ET$\to$0\% but only 24\% DR reduction on SciERC, compared to Full S1's 94\%.
25	The 70\,pp gap demonstrates that Level~1 constraints alone are insufficient for type-consistent IE regardless of implementation, motivating OntoCoDec's Level~2 semantic layer.
26	BoostCD additionally combines constrained and unconstrained decoding via boosting, a complementary strategy that could potentially be integrated with OntoCoDec's semantic layer.

_source line 6908 · 2026-05-21T03:31:48.596208+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}3$ schemas).
4	The two layers exhibit an enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning, \textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR (92--100\% reduction) with negligible RE R-F1 impact ($\pm$0.6\,pp) but introduces cross-field (CF) violations as a trade-off (0--21.1\% zero-shot, $\leq$1.3\% fine-tuned, dataset-dependent; \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
7	CF is substantially reduced by beam search under favorable conditions (low $\bar{\kappa}$ or fine-tuned settings).
8	The framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across four autoregressive LMs (7--70B; 70B as feasibility check in INT4 only, \S\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).
9	\end{abstract}
10

[Director 指令] [Patch] 两项文本修改，R16 后续补丁：

1. **Abstract "deployment-grade" 前提条件明确化**
当前 Abstract 使用 "deployment-grade" 但未说明前提。修改为明确标注：
- LoRA fine-tuning 是达到 ≤0.09% DR 的前提
- "calibrated models"（排除 Mistral 等未校准模型）是 zero-shot 高效果的前提
- 92-100% DR reduction 限于 fine-tuned setting
具体措辞可以是类似 "With LoRA fine-tuning on calibrated models, OntoCoDec achieves..." 的结构。

2. **DocRED 数据泄露澄清 (§5 Experimental Setup)**
在实验设置段落加一句澄清：schema constraints（domain/range type restrictions）来自 ontology 定义（如 Wikidata property constraints / SciERC schema specification），而非从评估数据中拟合。具体说明 constraints 的来源是 schema definition，与 evaluation data 独立。

_source line 6920 · 2026-05-21T03:37:21.908091+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}3$ schemas).
4	The two layers exhibit an enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning on well-calibrated models, \textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR (92--100\% reduction) with negligible RE R-F1 impact ($\pm$0.6\,pp) but introduces CF violations as a trade-off ($\leq$1.3\% fine-tuned); zero-shot effectiveness varies by dataset and model calibration (45--99\% DR reduction, 0--21.1\% CF; \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
7	CF is substantially reduced by beam search under favorable conditions (low $\bar{\kappa}$ or fine-tuned settings).
8	The framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across four autoregressive LMs (7--70B; 70B as feasibility check in INT4 only, \S\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).
9	\end{abstract}
10

5	\label{sec:violation_landscape}
6	
7	We evaluate OntoCoDec in two deployment configurations: LoRA + greedy S1 (\S\ref{sec:composability}), near-eliminating DR (DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%) with minimal overhead; and LoRA + beam S1 (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}), achieving zero total violations on SciERC under favorable conditions (1.54$\times$ latency).
8	Zero-shot evaluation (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}--\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) serves as a diagnostic probe to isolate constraint effects; R-F1 2--8 reflects absent task-specific training, not method failure.
9	The deployment-relevant evaluation is the fine-tuned setting (R-F1 40--46\%), where constraints compose with parameter optimization without degrading performance.
10	
11	\paragraph{Setup.}
12	We evaluate on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} (551 test sentences, 7 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$) and DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} (998 dev documents,\footnote{DocRED test annotations are not publicly available; we evaluate on the development set.} 96 relations, $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$).
13	RE type constraints are extracted automatically from observed type pairs in training data (neither dataset provides a formal ontology); EE constraints on WikiEvents (\S\ref{sec:wikievents}) are derived from the KAIROS ontology.
14	The framework handles both sources without modification.
15	Primary model: Qwen3-8B~\cite{qwen2025qwen3}, zero-shot greedy decoding; cross-model validation in \S\ref{sec:cross-model}, composability with fine-tuning in \S\ref{sec:composability}.
16	We measure four violation categories: \textbf{DR} (domain/range type pair $\notin \sigma(r)$), \textbf{RV} (relation $\notin \mathcal{R}$), \textbf{ET} (entity type $\notin \mathcal{E}$), and \textbf{CF} (same entity assigned conflicting types across triples).

…[truncated 284 chars]

_source line 6976 · 2026-05-21T03:41:10.239425+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Patch] Task E triple validity 数据写入论文

新实验结果需要集成到 §5 和 Abstract。核心数据：

**Lucky Violation Analysis（"so what" 论证）**:
- 约束消除的 violations 中，平均仅 0.49% 恰好匹配 gold labels（"lucky violations"）
- 因此 DR 15%→0.09% 的代价仅 <0.5% recall loss
- Llama SciERC FT 零 churn：所有 5 个 lucky violations 在 constrained 模式下被恢复为合法类型路径
- Precision@valid 基本持平（-0.20pp avg）

**具体数据**:
| Config | DR (unc→con) | Lucky Violations | Recall Δ | Churn |
|--------|-------------|-----------------|----------|-------|
| SciERC Qwen-FT | 1.95%→0% | 4 (0.41% gold) | -0.10pp | 0.22% |
| SciERC Llama-FT | 1.11%→0.09% | 5 (0.51% gold) | ±0.00pp | 0% |
| DocRED Qwen-FT | 1.03%→0.01% | 1 (0.01% gold) | -0.84pp | 2.05% |
| DocRED Llama-FT | 0.64%→0.21% | 1 (0.01% gold) | -0.10pp | 0.86% |

**写入位置**:
1. §5 新增一段 "Downstream Impact" 或 "Schema Compliance Value"，放在 ablation 讨论之后：论证约束带来 100% schema compliance 且几乎无 recall 代价，因为 violations 几乎不匹配 gold
2. Abstract 加一句 "with negligible recall cost (<0.5%)" 或类似表述，强化 "near-eliminates DR" 的实用价值
3. 可选：Appendix 加完整 per-config 表

**叙事要点**：这回答了 "R-F1 几乎没变那 DR 下降有什么用" 的问题。答案是：违规三元组几乎都不是正确的（lucky violation ≤0.5%），约束以极低代价换取 KG-ready output。

不需要验证引用。完成后通知我。

_source line 6980 · 2026-05-21T03:41:23.368441+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}3$ schemas).
4	The two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning on well-calibrated models, \textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR (92--100\% reduction) with negligible RE R-F1 impact ($\pm$0.6\,pp) but introduces CF violations as a trade-off ($\leq$1.3\% fine-tuned); zero-shot effectiveness varies by dataset and model calibration (45--99\% DR reduction, 0--21.1\% CF; \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
7	CF is substantially reduced by beam search under favorable conditions (low $\bar{\kappa}$ or fine-tuned settings).
8	The framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across four autoregressive LMs (7--70B; 70B as feasibility check in INT4 only, \S\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).
9	\end{abstract}
10

1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC and $+$2.43\% on DocRED (amortized over longer sequences); under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	Beam search ($k{=}4$), following the constrained beam search paradigm~\cite{anderson2017guided}, explores paths satisfying both layers jointly.
11	With LoRA, beam S1 eliminates all four violation types on SciERC for both models (R-F1 44--47); under zero-shot, beam reduces DR to 0\% and CF substantially (9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.53\% Qwen on SciERC; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
12	DocRED zero-shot beam evaluates on a 50-document subset (compute cost; LoRA beam uses the full 998-document set).
13	Total latency: 1.54$\times$ (beam 1.32$\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\times$; Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
14	
15	% exp_ids: 70b_scierc_unconstrained, 70b_scierc_constrained, 70b_docred_unconstrained, 70b_docred_constrained
16	\paragraph{Preliminary 70B evaluation.}
17	\label{sec:70b-results}
18	On Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), OntoCoDec achieves DR$\to$0\% on both SciERC (R-F1 $-$0.03\,pp) and DocRED (R-F1 $+$0.04\,pp), one of three configurations to eliminate DR on DocRED.
19	CF rises to 5.15--7.01\% (from 0.07\%), comparable to 8B zero-shot CF (2.57--9.90\%) and driven primarily by zero-shot type uncertainty rather than model scale; INT4 quantization may contribute.
20	Fine-tuning is the key CF mitigation: 8B FT reduces CF to 0.2--1.2\% (\S\ref{sec:composability}), suggesting a similar pattern would hold at 70B.
21	
22	\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
23	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}

4	\section{Extended Discussion}\label{app:discussion}

6	%%% CF Dual Mechanism %%%

8	
9	\paragraph{CF Dual Mechanism.}
10	CF violations arise from two distinct mechanisms:
11	(1)~\emph{Registry override}: the semantic constraint forces a domain-compatible type that conflicts with the entity's previously registered type.
12	The entity appears in multiple relations with different type requirements, and the per-relation mask selects the locally valid type without consulting prior assignments.
13	(2)~\emph{Grammar-first fallback} (\S\ref{sec:interaction}): when the semantic mask conflicts with all grammar-valid tokens, the semantic layer is dropped entirely, allowing the grammar layer to generate any structurally valid type, which may differ from the entity's registered type.
14	
15	These mechanisms have sharply different model profiles.
16	Qwen3-8B triggers zero grammar-first fallbacks across all experiments, attributing 100\% of its CF violations to registry override.
17	Llama-3.1-8B, by contrast, triggers 5{,}882 fallbacks on SciERC (10.7 per document) and 57{,}825 on DocRED (63.8 per document), making grammar-first fallback the dominant CF source.
18	This asymmetry is consistent with the cross-model CF gap: Qwen achieves CF\,=\,2.57\% on DocRED vs.\ Llama's 12.02\%, despite both using identical constraint configurations.
19	The difference traces to Llama's less calibrated type predictions, which more frequently conflict with the semantic mask's valid set, triggering fallback.
20	
21	%%% Fallback Behavior and Effective Coverage %%%
22	% exp_ids: cf_fallback_ablation_scierc (final data)
23

_source line 6988 · 2026-05-21T03:42:17.487847+00:00_

440	\paragraph{WikiEvents Dataset Details.}
441	\label{app:wikievents}
442	WikiEvents~\cite{li2021document} is a document-level event argument extraction benchmark grounded in the DARPA KAIROS ontology.
443	It defines 56 event types, 80 argument roles, and 27 entity types, with 245 (event\_type, role)$\to$allowed\_entity\_types constraints.
444	The grammar layer targets EV (event vocabulary), RoV (role vocabulary), and ET; the semantic layer enforces AC (argument constraint) via role-conditioned masking, analogous to RE domain/range enforcement.
445	
446	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness in WikiEvents.}
447	\label{app:wikievents-kappa}
448	% Data source: artifacts/w1c_wikievents_kappa_results.md
449	WikiEvents role-level tightness (with $|\mathcal{E}|{=}27$) has mean $\bar{\kappa}{=}0.82$ and median 0.89; 84\% of roles have $\kappa \geq 0.80$ (4 or fewer valid entity types out of 27).
450	The tightness-violation pattern extends to event extraction: roles with $\kappa \geq 0.90$ exhibit the highest AC rate (50\%), compared to 21\% for medium-tightness ($0.80 \leq \kappa < 0.90$) and 30\% for loose ($\kappa < 0.80$) roles; this is consistent with the RE observation that constraint tightness correlates with violation frequency.
451	However, the correlation is modulated by entity type prototypicality: semantically intuitive roles (e.g., Transportation.Vehicle$\to$VEH) show 0\% AC despite $\kappa{=}0.963$, while roles that require rare KAIROS-specific types (e.g., Crime$\to$CRM, Sentence$\to$SEN) show near-100\% AC because LLMs default to common types (PER, ORG).
452	The loose group's elevated rate (30\%) is driven by high-frequency roles such as Attack.Target (126 args, 34\% AC, $\kappa{=}0.59$), where systematic model errors outweigh the permissive constraint space.
453

1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC and $+$2.43\% on DocRED (amortized over longer sequences); under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	Beam search ($k{=}4$), following the constrained beam search paradigm~\cite{anderson2017guided}, explores paths satisfying both layers jointly.
11	With LoRA, beam S1 eliminates all four violation types on SciERC for both models (R-F1 44--47); under zero-shot, beam reduces DR to 0\% and CF substantially (9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.53\% Qwen on SciERC; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
12	DocRED zero-shot beam evaluates on a 50-document subset (compute cost; LoRA beam uses the full 998-document set).
13	Total latency: 1.54$\times$ (beam 1.32$\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\times$; Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
14	
15	% exp_ids: 70b_scierc_unconstrained, 70b_scierc_constrained, 70b_docred_unconstrained, 70b_docred_constrained
16	\paragraph{Preliminary 70B evaluation.}
17	\label{sec:70b-results}
18	On Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), OntoCoDec achieves DR$\to$0\% on both SciERC (R-F1 $-$0.03\,pp) and DocRED (R-F1 $+$0.04\,pp), one of three configurations to eliminate DR on DocRED.
19	CF rises to 5.15--7.01\% (from 0.07\%), comparable to 8B zero-shot CF (2.57--9.90\%) and driven primarily by zero-shot type uncertainty rather than model scale; INT4 quantization may contribute.
20	Fine-tuning is the key CF mitigation: 8B FT reduces CF to 0.2--1.2\% (\S\ref{sec:composability}), suggesting a similar pattern would hold at 70B.
21

_source line 7062 · 2026-05-21T03:49:49.504267+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Patch] GoLLIE/KnowCoder 互补性讨论（非 head-to-head 对比）

GoLLIE head-to-head 无法实现——GoLLIE Python dataclass 格式与 JSON constrained decoding 不兼容，不存在"同 prompt 同 model"的可控对比。改为互补性讨论，放在 §6 Related Work 或 §5 Discussion 中。

**可用数据点**：
- GoLLIE (CodeLlama-7B, ZS, SciERC 100 docs): DR=0%, CF=0.67%, R-F1=0%, 45% 空输出 — 格式合规但抽取能力极弱
- KnowCoder (LLaMA2-7B, SciERC): R-F1=40.0 (published) vs OntoCoDec FT+S1 R-F1=45.47
- OntoCoDec trie baseline (同模型同数据): 结果待补充（实验在跑）

**叙事要点**（1-2 句，§6 Related Work 中 constrained decoding 段落）：
- GoLLIE 和 KnowCoder 通过 prompt 格式约束（code-style schemas）实现结构合规，但不强制语义约束（domain/range types）。OntoCoDec 与之互补：在保持抽取质量的前提下强制 runtime semantic compliance。
- 明确声明：不同模型、不同范式，非 controlled comparison。直接引用 KnowCoder R-F1=40.0 作为竞争力参考。

**不要做的事**：
- 不要声称 "OntoCoDec > GoLLIE"（不公平对比）
- 不要把 D063 的 R-F1=0% 作为 GoLLIE 方法的评价（那是 ZS CodeLlama 在 SciERC 上不工作，不是 GoLLIE 方法的问题）

不需要验证引用。完成后通知我。

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	We provide a detailed survey in Appendix~\ref{app:related_work}; here we position OntoCoDec along three axes.
5	
6	\paragraph{Grammar-constrained decoding.}
7	XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, Outlines~\cite{willard2023outlines}, SynCode~\cite{ugare2024syncode}, and related systems~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql,park2024grammar,jsonschemabench2025} enforce format validity via CFG-based token masking.
8	In code generation, PICARD~\cite{scholak2021picard} and \citet{pldi2025_type_constrained_codegen} compile type systems into prefix automata, but IE type environments accumulate dynamically (\S\ref{sec:composition}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Schema-aware IE.}
11	GoLLIE~\cite{sainz2024gollie}, KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024}, GUIDEX~\cite{guidex2025}, GenIE~\cite{josifoski2022genie}, TANL~\cite{paolini2021tanl}, REBEL~\cite{cabot2021rebel}, InstructUIE~\cite{wang2023instructuie}, and others~\cite{fei2022lasuie,wang2022deepstruct,lu2022uie,lou2023usm,zhou2024universalner} encode ontology structure or frame IE as structured generation, though schema compliance gaps persist~\cite{wadhwa2023revisiting}.
12	
13	\paragraph{Constraint enforcement in IE.}
14	\citet{roth_yih_2004} and \citet{riedel2010modeling} formulated hard type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation~\cite{khorshidi2025odke,spires_2024,ontologx_2025} detects violations after generation; EnriCo~\cite{zaratiana2024enrico}, IterGen~\cite{ugare2025itergen}, and DCCD~\cite{dccd2026} enforce constraints via ASP, backtracking, or draft-conditioning.
15	Grammar-only enforcement (GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd}, BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}) achieves only partial DR reduction; the quantitative gap is analyzed in \S\ref{sec:constrained-baselines}.
16	OntoCoDec uses \emph{preventive} forward-only token masking at $O(|V|)$ per step; while constrained decoding can degrade reasoning~\cite{banerjee2025crane,schall2025hiddencost,formattax2026}, extraction tasks are robust to format co

1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	
3	OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a decidability-guided two-layer architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% ($<$0.5\% recall cost), CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\% for calibrated models (primarily 7--8B; preliminary 70B in \S\ref{sec:70b-results}).
4

_source line 7092 · 2026-05-21T04:59:53.919234+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Patch] Trie baseline 数据写入 §5 — MF1 关键回应

**这是连续 5 轮 MF1 (baseline comparison) 的核心回应数据。**

Trie-only（GCD-equivalent vocabulary membership constraints，无 JSON grammar enforcement）在所有 4 个 SciERC 配置上比 unconstrained 更差：

| Config | Unconstrained DR% | Trie-only DR% | Grammar+Semantic DR% |
|--------|-------------------|---------------|---------------------|
| Qwen FT | 2.64 | 95.51 | 0.0 |
| Qwen ZS | 15.37 | 29.26 | 0.93 |
| Llama FT | 1.11 | 15.21 (+50.8% parse fail) | 0.09 |
| Llama ZS | ~15 | 84.42 | ~0.93 |

**ET=0%, RV=0% across all 4 configs** — trie correctly constrains vocabulary when it can, but without grammar enforcement, models omit type fields (83%) or produce malformed JSON (0-51% parse failure).

**写入位置**：§5 BoostCD/GCD 对比段落旁（或替换），新增 3-4 行：

推荐措辞方向：
"Trie-only constraints (vocabulary membership without JSON structure enforcement, functionally equivalent to GCD's constrained decoding) produce DR rates of 15–96%—*worse* than unconstrained baselines (1–15%)—because models omit entity type fields or generate malformed JSON without grammar-level structural enforcement. This confirms the grammar layer is a prerequisite for vocabulary constraints to operate (Table X)."

**数据呈现**：
- 在现有 Table 2 或新增小表中加 "Trie-only (GCD-equiv.)" 行
- 或者在 Appendix 放完整表，主文用行内数据

**叙事要点**：这不是"我们的方法比 GCD 好"的对比——而是"vocabulary-level constraints without grammar are actively harmful"的 enabling dependency 证据。强调 trie ET=0%/RV=0% 说明 vocabulary 约束本身正确，问题在于缺少结构性强制。

不需要验证引用。完成后通知我。

1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC and $+$2.43\% on DocRED (amortized over longer sequences); under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	Beam search ($k{=}4$)~\cite{anderson2017guided} explores paths satisfying both layers jointly.
11	With LoRA, beam S1 eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 44--47); zero-shot beam reduces DR to 0\% and CF substantially (9.90\%\,$\to$\,0.53\% Qwen; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
12	DocRED zero-shot beam evaluates on a 50-document subset (compute cost; LoRA beam uses the full 998-document set).
13	Total latency: 1.54$\times$ (beam 1.32$\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\times$; Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
14	
15	% exp_ids: 70b_scierc_unconstrained, 70b_scierc_constrained, 70b_docred_unconstrained, 70b_docred_constrained
16	\paragraph{Preliminary 70B evaluation.}
17	\label{sec:70b-results}
18	On Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), OntoCoDec achieves DR$\to$0\% on both SciERC (R-F1 $-$0.03\,pp) and DocRED (R-F1 $+$0.04\,pp), one of three configurations to eliminate DR on DocRED.
19	CF rises to 5.15--7.01\% (from 0.07\%), comparable to 8B zero-shot CF (2.57--9.90\%) and driven primarily by zero-shot type uncertainty; fine-tuning reduces 8B CF to 0.2--1.2\% (\S\ref{sec:composability}), suggesting a similar pattern at 70B.

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}\n\\label{sec:constrained-baselines}\nOur grammar-only ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same class of constraints---vocabulary membership via context-free grammars---as grammar-constrained decoding systems such as GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} and the grammar component of BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}.\nThis ablation achieves RV/ET$\\to$0\\% but only 24\\% DR reduction on SciERC, compared to Full S1's 94\\%.\nThe 70\\,pp gap demonstrates that Level~1 constraints alone are insufficient for type-consistent IE regardless of implementation, motivating OntoCoDec's Level~2 semantic layer.\nBoostCD additionally combines constrained and unconstrained decoding via boosting, a complementary strategy that could potentially be integrated with OntoCoDec's semantic layer.",
  "new_string": "% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc\n\\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}\n\\label{sec:constrained-baselines}\nOur grammar-only ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same class of constraints---vocabulary membership via context-free grammars---as GCD~\\cite{geng2023gcd} and the grammar component of BoostCD~\\cite{boostcd2025}, achieving RV/ET$\\to$0\\% but only 24\\% DR reduction vs.\\ Full S1's 94\\%.\nWithout JSON structural enforcement, trie-only vocabulary constraints (GCD-equivalent) are counterproductive: DR rises to 15--96\\% (vs.\\ 1--15\\% unconstrained) because models omit type fields or produce malformed JSON without grammar-level structure (Appendix~\\ref{app:trie-baseline}), confirming both layers are necessary.\nBoostCD additionally combines constrained and unconstrained decoding via boosting, a complementary strategy potentially integrable with OntoCoDec's semantic layer."
}

456	
457	\paragraph{Lucky Violation Analysis.}
458	\label{app:lucky-violations}
459	Table~\ref{tab:lucky-violations} quantifies the recall cost of DR elimination.
460	A ``lucky violation'' is a constraint-removed triple that happens to match a gold label---an ontologically invalid prediction that is nonetheless correct.
461	Across four fine-tuned configurations, lucky violations average 0.49\% of gold triples (range 0.01--0.51\%), confirming that nearly all removed DR violations are false positives.
462	On Llama SciERC, all five lucky violations are recovered as legal type paths under constrained decoding (zero churn): the model finds an ontology-compliant route to the same prediction.
463	
464	\begin{table}[h]
465	\centering
466	\small
467	\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrr@{}}
468	\toprule
469	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Dataset} & \textbf{DR (\%)} & \textbf{Lucky} & \textbf{$\Delta$Recall} & \textbf{Churn} \\
470	& & \textbf{unc$\to$con} & \textbf{(\% gold)} & \textbf{(pp)} & \textbf{(\%)} \\
471	\midrule
472	Qwen3-8B  & SciERC & 1.95$\to$0    & 4 (0.41\%) & $-$0.10 & 0.22 \\
473	Llama-8B  & SciERC & 1.11$\to$0.09 & 5 (0.51\%) & $\pm$0.00 & 0 \\
474	Qwen3-8B  & DocRED & 1.03$\to$0.01 & 1 (0.01\%) & $-$0.84 & 2.05 \\
475	Llama-8B  & DocRED & 0.64$\to$0.21 & 1 (0.01\%) & $-$0.10 & 0.86 \\
476	\midrule
477	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textit{Average}} & & \textit{0.49\%} & $-$0.26 & 0.78 \\
478	\bottomrule
479	\end{tabular}

  "new_string": "\\label{tab:lucky-violations}\n\\end{table}\n\n%%% Trie-Only Baseline %%%\n% exp_ids: trie_qwen_ft_scierc, trie_qwen_zs_scierc, trie_llama_ft_scierc, trie_llama_zs_scierc\n\n\\paragraph{Trie-Only Baseline (GCD-Equivalent).}\n\\label{app:trie-baseline}\nTable~\\ref{tab:trie-baseline} isolates the effect of vocabulary membership constraints without JSON structural enforcement.\nThe trie constrains entity type and relation tokens to schema-valid values (achieving ET${=}$0\\%, RV${=}$0\\% across all configurations), functionally equivalent to GCD's~\\cite{geng2023gcd} constrained decoding.\nHowever, without grammar-level JSON enforcement, models systematically omit entity type fields (83\\% of triples) or produce malformed JSON (0--51\\% parse failure), causing DR to rise to 15--96\\%---far worse than unconstrained baselines.\nThis confirms the grammar layer is a structural prerequisite: vocabulary constraints operate correctly in isolation but require JSON scaffolding to reach the positions where they apply.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Unconstr.} & \\textbf{Trie-only} & \\textbf{Full S1} \\\\\n& & \\textbf{DR\\,(\\%)} & \\textbf{DR\\,(\\%)} & \\textbf{DR\\,(\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & LoRA  &  2.64 & 95.51 & \\textbf{0} \\\\\n  & ZS    & 15.37 & 29.26 & \\textbf{0.93} \\\\\n\\multirow{2}{*}{Llama-8B}\n  & LoRA  &  1.11 & 15.21$^\\dagger$ & \\textbf{0.09} \\\\\n  & ZS    & $\\sim$15 & 84.42 & $\\sim$\\textbf{0.93} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Trie-only (vocabulary membership without JSON grammar) vs.\\ unconstrained and Full S1 on SciERC.\nTrie-only achieves ET${=}$0\\%, RV${=}$0\\% but DR worsens because models omit type fields without structural enforcement.\n$^\\dagger$50.8\\% parse failure rate.}\n\\label{tab:trie-baseline}\n\\end{table}"

22	\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
23	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}
24	Our grammar-only ablation (Table~\ref{tab:scierc-ablation}, row~2) implements the same class of constraints---vocabulary membership via context-free grammars---as GCD~\cite{geng2023gcd} and the grammar component of BoostCD~\cite{boostcd2025}, achieving RV/ET$\to$0\% but only 24\% DR reduction vs.\ Full S1's 94\%.
25	Without JSON structural enforcement, trie-only vocabulary constraints (GCD-equivalent) are counterproductive: DR rises to 15--96\% (vs.\ 1--15\% unconstrained) because models omit type fields or produce malformed JSON without grammar-level structure (Appendix~\ref{app:trie-baseline}), confirming both layers are necessary.
26	BoostCD additionally combines constrained and unconstrained decoding via boosting, a complementary strategy potentially integrable with OntoCoDec's semantic layer.
27

3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC and $+$2.43\% on DocRED (amortized over longer sequences); under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.

_source line 7124 · 2026-05-21T05:43:54.462813+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}3$ schemas).
4	The two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning on well-calibrated models, \textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR (92--100\% reduction, $<$0.5\% true-positive loss) with negligible RE R-F1 impact ($\pm$0.6\,pp) but introduces CF violations as a trade-off ($\leq$1.3\% fine-tuned); zero-shot effectiveness varies by dataset and model calibration (45--99\% DR reduction, 0--21.1\% CF; \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
7	CF is substantially reduced by beam search under favorable conditions (low $\bar{\kappa}$ or fine-tuned settings).
8	The framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across four autoregressive LMs (7--70B; 70B as feasibility check in INT4 only, \S\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).
9	\end{abstract}
10

_source line 7128 · 2026-05-21T05:44:40.316142+00:00_

1	\section{Beam Search Compatibility}
2	\label{app:beam-search}
3	
4	OntoCoDec's constraint enforcement is designed to be decoding-strategy agnostic.
5	The main experiments (\S\ref{sec:experiments}) use greedy decoding; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling} validates nucleus sampling.
6	Here we verify compatibility with beam search, completing coverage of the three standard decoding strategies.
7	
8	\paragraph{Implementation challenges.}
9	Two stateful components require adaptation for beam search.
10	(1)~The grammar layer (XGrammar) maintains an internal \texttt{GrammarMatcher} state that tracks the generated token sequence; beam reordering invalidates this state because beams are permuted across steps.
11	Our solution instantiates a fresh \texttt{GrammarMatcher} per beam per step and replays the beam's current token sequence through XGrammar's stateless accept API (64 lines of code).
12	(2)~The semantic layer's \texttt{TypeConstraintProcessor} accumulates entity and relation registries that assume a single generation path.
13	Our solution isolates state per beam by calling \texttt{detect()} on the full partial output at each step, reconstructing all entity/relation state from scratch for each beam independently (61 lines of code).
14	Both adaptations add ${\sim}125$ lines total without modifying the existing greedy/sampling logic.
15	
16	\paragraph{Results.}

84	Scaling to smaller models has not been verified, and the dual-factor pattern's predictive scope may differ at other scales.
85	Composability with fine-tuned models is validated on SciERC, DocRED, and WikiEvents with Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B (RE: DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\%, R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp; EE: RoV$\to$0\%, AC$\leq$0.8\%), but evaluation is limited to one fine-tuning method (LoRA); residual CF (Qwen 1.18\%, Llama 0.46\% on SciERC) traces to schema-level type ambiguity (\textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X) rather than enforcement failure.
86	WikiEvents fine-tuning composability is preliminary (20-document test set, the full official split).
87	All main experiments use greedy decoding; our evaluation of beam search on the full SciERC test set (551 documents) and DocRED (50 zero-shot, 998 fine-tuned documents) across three models confirms compatibility (Appendices~\ref{app:sampling},~\ref{app:beam-search}), but comprehensive evaluation across all decoding configurations remains future work.
88	
89	\paragraph{Engineering considerations.}
90	Scalability benchmarks up to 200 entity types and 1,000 relations show constant 2--3\% inference overhead and sub-second compilation ($<$0.5\,s; Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}); however, the first-token uniqueness requirement for continuation masking may become a tokenizer-dependent bottleneck beyond this scale.
91	Our approach requires access to model logits for token-level masking, which excludes API-only models (e.g., GPT-4, Claude) where only output text is available; extending to API-only settings would require alternative enforcement mechanisms such as constrained sampling APIs or output reranking.
92	Continuation masking assumes each entity type name maps to a distinguishable token prefix sequence.

_source line 7144 · 2026-05-21T05:46:59.061268+00:00_

[Director 指令] [Patch — 补充] Beam CF claim scoping

Reviewer 指出 Qwen DocRED beam S1 CF=4.94% 比 greedy S1 CF=2.57% 更高（beam 增加了 CF）。只有 Llama 展示 CF 下降。

在写 beam 段落时确保：
1. **不要笼统声称 "beam reduces CF"** — 这只对部分配置成立
2. DR=0% 是 beam 的核心 claim，CF 行为是 model-dependent
3. 如果 Abstract 或 §5 有 "beam substantially reduces CF" 类似表述，改为更精确的 scoping，如 "beam eliminates residual DR; CF effects are model-dependent (Table X)"

这是防止审稿人攻击的预防性修改。

_source line 7146 · 2026-05-21T05:47:06.732154+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}3$ schemas).
4	The two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning on well-calibrated models, \textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR (92--100\% reduction, $<$0.5\% true-positive loss) with negligible RE R-F1 impact ($\pm$0.6\,pp) but introduces CF violations as a trade-off ($\leq$1.3\% fine-tuned); zero-shot effectiveness varies by dataset and model calibration (45--99\% DR reduction, 0--21.1\% CF; \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
7	CF is substantially reduced by beam search under favorable conditions (low $\bar{\kappa}$ or fine-tuned settings).
8	The framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across four autoregressive LMs (7--70B; 70B as feasibility check in INT4 only, \S\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).
9	\end{abstract}
10

3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC and $+$2.43\% on DocRED (amortized over longer sequences); under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.

18	A decidability analysis of IE type constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability}) yields a two-layer architecture: the \emph{grammar layer} compiles ontology constraints into context-free grammars (JSON structure, vocabulary membership) via XGrammar; the \emph{semantic layer} interprets type constraints at runtime via dynamic logit masking over an entity registry, with an \emph{empirical enabling dependency} between layers.
19	OntoCoDec compiles constraints automatically from IE ontologies, requires no constraint-specific training, and is applicable to autoregressive LMs with logits access; we validate on three English datasets (up to 96 relation types), four models (7--70B), and three tokenizer families (Llama, Qwen, Mistral).
20	DR enforcement is precision-preserving but introduces CF side-effects (\S\ref{sec:scierc-results}); fine-tuning resolves most CF ($\leq$1.3\%).
21	LoRA + greedy S1 near-eliminates DR ($\leq$0.09\%) with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp and $+$10\% latency; beam S1 ($k{=}4$) additionally reduces CF (1.54$\times$ latency).
22	Zero-shot evaluation on 7--8B models with preliminary 70B results confirms DR drops by 76--99\% for models with well-calibrated type predictions (excluding Mistral-7B due to atypical entity typing interaction; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}).
23	
24	We make a systematic engineering contribution with theoretical grounding:
25	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
26	    \item A two-layer constrained decoding framework: we formalize a well-known intuition that cross-field type constraints require runtime state beyond context-free expressiveness (Proposition~1), yielding pair-based sequential enforcement at each level (\S\ref{sec:decidability}). Constraints compile automatically from IE ontologies, validated on schemas of varying restrictiveness with up to 96 relation types.

_source line 7158 · 2026-05-21T05:48:11.640166+00:00_

23	\paragraph{Greedy limitation and fallback cascade.}
24	\label{sec:greedy-limitation}
25	The semantic layer commits entity types greedily---an instance of Level~3 (non-local) constraints (\S\ref{sec:decidability})---so CF violations arise when the same entity receives different types across triples; beam search substantially reduces this (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
26	At semantically meaningful positions (first token of each type field), grammar-first fallback triggers on 0.5--5.7\% of positions under zero-shot decoding, correlating with $\bar{\kappa}$ (SciERC $\leq$\,0.5\%; DocRED 1.4--5.7\%); the higher token-level rate (36.6\%; Appendix~\ref{app:fallback-coverage}) reflects multi-token type name continuations.
27	Fine-tuning virtually eliminates fallback ($\leq$\,0.11\%): all zero-shot fallback originates from model preference conflicts, so fine-tuning aligns output distributions with schema-consistent types, transitioning the semantic layer from primary enforcer to safety net.

15	\paragraph{Preliminary 70B evaluation.}
16	\label{sec:70b-results}
17	On Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), OntoCoDec achieves DR$\to$0\% on both SciERC (R-F1 $-$0.03\,pp) and DocRED (R-F1 $+$0.04\,pp), one of three configurations to eliminate DR on DocRED.
18	CF rises to 5.15--7.01\% (from 0.07\%), comparable to 8B zero-shot CF (2.57--9.90\%) and driven primarily by zero-shot type uncertainty; fine-tuning reduces 8B CF to 0.2--1.2\% (\S\ref{sec:composability}), suggesting a similar pattern at 70B.

21	\paragraph{Comparison with grammar-constrained approaches.}
22	\label{sec:constrained-baselines}

[Director 指令] [Patch] Figure 1 文字与箭头/方框重叠修复

用户反馈：Figure 1 (fig1_framework.tex) 中文字与箭头、方框有多处重叠。当前整个 3-section 架构图用 `\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}` 缩放到单栏宽度，导致：

**已知问题**：
1. Section 1 "compile" 注释 (2.6, 6.9) 与 onto→jschema/tpairs 箭头重叠
2. Grammar/Semantic layer boxes 之间的 "enabling" 标签与 dashed arrow 挤在一起
3. Section 3 diamond "G∩S=∅?" 与 "No"/"Yes" 标签及 Sample/Fallback boxes 距离太近
4. 自回归循环箭头 "$t+1$" 标签与其他元素重叠
5. 各 box 内的文字在缩放后过小，与 box 边框挤压

**修复方向**（选一个或组合）：
- **方案 A**：改为 `figure*`（双栏全宽），给更多横向空间。需要调整节点坐标。
- **方案 B**：保持单栏，但精简图中文字（去掉部分注释、缩短标签），增大节点间距。
- **方案 C**：保持单栏，调整各 node 位置和 label anchor，增加 inner sep，让 label 不与 arrow 重叠。

推荐方案 A（`figure*` 全宽），因为图有 3 个 section + 大量箭头和注释，单栏放不下不重叠。改为全宽后重新调整坐标使三个 section 均匀分布。

修改后编译确认无重叠，通知我。

=== PAGE 9 full ===
630   Constraint enforcement in IE. Roth and Yih
631   (2004) and Riedel et al. (2010) formulated hard
632   type constraints via ILP; post-hoc validation (Khor-
633   shidi et al., 2025; Caufield et al., 2024; Cotti
634   et al., 2025) detects violations after generation;
635   EnriCo (Zaratiana et al., 2024), IterGen (Ugare
636   et al., 2025), and DCCD (Reddy et al., 2026)
637   enforce constraints via ASP, backtracking, or
638   draft-conditioning. Grammar-only enforcement
639   (GCD (Geng et al., 2023), BoostCD (Sakota et al.,
640   2025)) achieves only partial DR reduction (§5.4).
641   OntoCoDec uses forward-only token masking at
642   O(|V |) per step; extraction tasks are robust to for-
643   mat constraints (Tam et al., 2024) despite reasoning
644   degradation in other domains (Banerjee et al., 2025;
645   Lee et al., 2026).

646   7   Discussion and Conclusion
647   OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into compos-
648   able token-level constraints via a two-layer archi-
649   tecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR ≤ 0.09%
650   (<0.5% recall cost), CF ≤ 1.2%; beam (k=4) elim-
651   inates all violations on SciERC (1.54× latency);
652   zero-shot DR drops 76–99% for calibrated models
653   (7–8B; preliminary 70B in §5.4).

647   OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into compos-

70	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
71	\paragraph{Zero-shot diagnostic.}
72	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093
73	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836
74	% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824
75	Full S1 reduces DR from 15.37\% to 0.93\% ($\downarrow$94\%) while R-F1 is unchanged (8.23\,$\to$\,8.36, $p{=}0.58$).
76	Per-relation DR drops to $\leq$2.82\%, reaching 0\% on 4/7 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
77	Post-hoc filtering discards up to 48\% of predictions rather than correcting them (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-extended}); CF rises to 9.90\% (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
78	
79	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
80	%  (b) Grammar layer
81	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
82	\paragraph{Layer contributions.}
83	% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823
84	Grammar-only eliminates RV and ET with no F1 impact while DR remains at 14.84\%, the ceiling of grammar-based decoding~\cite{geng2023gcd}; grammar-only CF is near-zero (0.08\%), so CF originates from the semantic layer.
85	Semantic-only reduces DR by only 24\% and cannot eliminate RV or ET; this is the \emph{empirical enabling dependency}: adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\% reduction (11.65\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
86	
87	
88	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
89	%  (d) S1 vs S2
90	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
91	\paragraph{S1 vs.\ S2.}
92	S2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\ 8.36) without resolving CF (8.03\% vs.\ 9.90\%); we adopt S1.

1	% Section: Constraint Composition (compressed, 787→~500 words)
2	
3	At each decoding step $t$, let $G(t) \subseteq V$ and $S(t) \subseteq V$ denote the tokens permitted by the grammar and semantic layers, respectively.
4	The final mask is their conjunction:
5	%
6	\begin{equation}\label{eq:composition}
7	  M_{\text{final}}(t) = M_{\text{grammar}}(t) \;\wedge\; M_{\text{semantic}}(t),
8	\end{equation}
9	%
10	where $\wedge$ denotes element-wise AND over the vocabulary $V$; a token is allowed only if it satisfies \emph{both} structural and type-level constraints.
11	The conjunction is asymmetric: $S(t)$ is defined only over $G(t)$. This reflects the empirical enabling dependency (\S\ref{sec:method}): the semantic layer requires the relation type to belong to $\mathcal{R}$, a property enforced only by the grammar layer.
12	
13	\paragraph{Grammar-First Fallback.}
14	When $S(t) \cap G(t) = \emptyset$ (tight constraints leave no token that is both grammar-valid and semantically valid), na\"{\i}ve masking would force selection of a grammar-invalid token, breaking JSON structure.
15	We enforce a \emph{grammar-first fallback}: semantic masking is skipped and the grammar layer alone controls selection.
16	Structural integrity is a hard constraint; type violations are soft errors detectable by downstream validation, with DR (ontologically invalid type pair) more severe than CF (individually valid triples with cross-triple type inconsistency).
17	
18	\paragraph{Constraint Tightness.}
19	\label{eq:tightness}
20	We define per-relation constraint tightness $\kappa(r) = 1 - |\sigma(r)|/|\mathcal{E}|^2$ and the schema-level mean $\bar{\kappa} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}\sum_{r} \kappa(r)$.
21	Across three schemas---WikiEvents ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.029$; 27 entity types, 56 event types, mean 21 valid type pairs per event type out of $27^2{=}729$ possible), SciERC ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.333$), DocRED ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$)---DR reduction ranges from 94--99\% (low $\bar{\kappa}$) to 42--90\% 
…[truncated 1680 chars]

_source line 7260 · 2026-05-21T11:13:12.402666+00:00_

53	
54	\paragraph{Results.}
55	Unconstrained EE produces extreme vocabulary violations (EV+RoV $>$85\%; Table~\ref{tab:wikievents}), with entity F1\,=\,0 because the parser cannot process off-schema output.
56	Full S1 eliminates EV and RoV across all models, which validates the tokenizer-agnostic design across byte-level BPE and SentencePiece.
57	Grammar-only eliminates EV but leaves RoV at 64--77\% and surfaces latent AC (8--14\%), consistent with DocRED's pattern (\S\ref{sec:docred-results}); full S1 drives RoV to 0\%.
58	Residual AC (1.3--3.8\%) traces to semantically similar types (e.g., SID vs.\ SEN).
59	CF rises to 15--21\%, exceeding RE levels (10--12\%) because EE's multi-argument structures increase entity reuse across slots.
60	Post-hoc filtering is impractical ($<$0.5\% arguments retained).
61	Unlike RE, EE shows measurable F1 gains: entity F1 0\%\,$\to$\,12--18\%, trigger F1 $+$1.5--1.8\,pp.
62	
63	% exp_ids: p1_ee_ft_qwen_wikievents_r5, p1_ee_ft_llama_wikievents_r5
64	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
65	We evaluate on the full official test split (20 documents; 194/20/20~\cite{li2021document}); the small test set is an inherent dataset limitation, not a subset choice.
66	LoRA + S1 eliminates RoV (Qwen 38.31\%\,$\to$\,0\%, Llama 8.8\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and reduces AC to $\leq$0.8\%, with argument F1 within $\pm$1.1\,pp (CIs highly overlapping; B${=}$1000); trigger F1 shifts ($-$5.4/$-$0.6\,pp) have wide CIs consistent with n${=}$20.
67	CF remains low (0.77--1.18\%), consistent with RE (\S\ref{sec:composition}); the semantic layer enforces per-event role-type compatibility but not cross-event entity type consensus.
68	Per-document variance is high (Arg-F1 std/mean ${>}$\,0.5, 35--47\% of documents scoring zero), confirming sufficient power to detect violation elimination but not small F1 differences.
69

1	% Runtime overhead (inline paragraph, no subsection header to save space)
2	
3	\paragraph{Runtime overhead.}
4	\label{sec:runtime}
5	S1 overhead is $+$13.1\% on SciERC and $+$2.43\% on DocRED (amortized over longer sequences); under LoRA, $+$10.0\% with 37\% lower absolute latency.
6	Constraint compilation is one-time $<$0.5\,s; scaling benchmarks confirm constant overhead (2--3\%) up to 200 entity types and 1{,}000 relations (Appendix~\ref{app:scalability}).
7	
8	\paragraph{Beam search compatibility.}
9	\label{sec:beam-results}
10	Beam search ($k{=}4$)~\cite{anderson2017guided} explores paths satisfying both layers jointly.
11	With LoRA, beam S1 eliminates all four violation types on SciERC (R-F1 44--47); zero-shot beam achieves DR${=}$0\% on both datasets (998-doc DocRED; CF response is model-dependent; Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
12	Total latency: 1.54$\times$ (beam 1.32$\times$ $+$ constraint 1.17$\times$; Table~\ref{tab:beam-latency}).
13	
14	% exp_ids: 70b_scierc_unconstrained, 70b_scierc_constrained, 70b_docred_unconstrained, 70b_docred_constrained
15	\paragraph{Preliminary 70B evaluation.}
16	\label{sec:70b-results}
17	On Llama-3.1-70B (AWQ INT4), OntoCoDec achieves DR$\to$0\% on both SciERC (R-F1 $+$0.09\,pp) and DocRED (R-F1 $+$0.04\,pp), one of three configurations to eliminate DR on DocRED.
18	CF rises to 3.50--7.01\% (from $\leq$0.07\%), comparable to 8B zero-shot CF (2.57--9.90\%) and driven primarily by zero-shot type uncertainty; fine-tuning reduces 8B CF to 0.2--1.2\% (\S\ref{sec:composability}), suggesting a similar pattern at 70B.
19

1	% Section 7 — Discussion and Conclusion
2	
3	OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a two-layer architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% ($<$0.5\% recall cost), CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\% for calibrated models (7--8B; preliminary 70B in \S\ref{sec:70b-results}).
4

68	% === Limitations (ARR requirement: standalone section, does not count toward page limit) ===
69	\clearpage
70	\section*{Limitations}
71	
72	\paragraph{Constraint scope.}
73	CF impact is model-dependent: for well-calibrated models (Qwen, Llama; DR\,$\leq$\,1\% under greedy constrained decoding), S1 introduces CF violations (up to 12.02\%) as a consequence of per-relation independent enforcement; for poorly-calibrated models (Mistral), S1 substantially reduces CF (56.87\%\,$\to$\,1.27\%) by constraining type assignments to schema-valid values.
74	CF fallback ablation shows that eliminating CF re-routes violations to DR ($+$13.85\,pp). CF$\leftrightarrow$DR thus represents a fundamental trade-off in composable constraint systems rather than an independently resolvable limitation (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-dr-tradeoff}).
75	Global entity-level type consensus during decoding offers the most direct path to resolving CF violations.
76	CF type inconsistencies may propagate to downstream tasks that consume extracted triples (e.g., knowledge graph construction, question answering); the impact of such propagation has not been evaluated.
77	Entity F1 impact is model-dependent ($+$1.22\,pp for Qwen vs.\ $-$0.64\,pp for Llama on DocRED), partially confounded by increased parse failures under constraints (Llama: 9.1\% vs.\ 6.8\%).
78	Residual DR under fine-tuning (36 violations across all fine-tuned settings, both models and both datasets) decomposes into entity type errors (39\%), schema coverage gaps (33\%), and model generation errors correctly caught by constraints (28\%); Llama DocRED alone accounts for 33 of 36 residual violations (92\%), reflecting weaker entity typing (Appendix~\ref{app:residual-dr-analysis}).

_source line 7312 · 2026-05-22T03:43:20.953270+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	We present \textsc{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates Information Extraction (IE) ontologies into composable token-level decoding constraints via a decidability-guided architecture: a \emph{grammar layer} (context-free) compiles ontology constraints into grammars ensuring JSON validity and vocabulary membership; a \emph{semantic layer} (prefix-decidable with state) interprets type constraints at runtime via stateful token masking over a dynamic entity registry, with grammar-first fallback when constraints conflict.
3	Without constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations that persist after fine-tuning, with rates characterized by \emph{constraint tightness} ($\bar{\kappa}$), a descriptive metric correlating schema restrictiveness with enforcement outcomes ($n{=}3$ schemas).
4	The two layers exhibit an empirical enabling dependency (Proposition~1): neither achieves this reduction alone.
5	% exp_ids: w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval, w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval
6	Composed with LoRA fine-tuning on well-calibrated models, \textsc{OntoCoDec} near-eliminates DR (92--100\% reduction, $<$0.5\% true-positive loss) with negligible RE R-F1 impact ($\pm$0.6\,pp) but introduces CF violations as a trade-off ($\leq$1.3\% fine-tuned); zero-shot effectiveness varies by dataset and model calibration (45--99\% DR reduction, 0--21.1\% CF; \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
7	Beam search eliminates residual DR; CF effects are configuration-dependent, with full elimination on SciERC under LoRA (\S\ref{sec:beam-results}).
8	The framework is validated on RE (SciERC, DocRED) with preliminary event extraction results on WikiEvents, across five autoregressive LMs (7--70B; two 70B as feasibility checks, \S\ref{sec:70b-results}) with logits access and three tokenizer families (up to 96 relation types).
9	\end{abstract}
10

3	OntoCoDec translates IE ontologies into composable token-level constraints via a two-layer architecture: LoRA + greedy S1 achieves DR\,$\leq$\,0.09\% ($<$0.5\% recall cost), CF\,$\leq$\,1.2\%; beam ($k{=}4$) eliminates all violations on SciERC (1.54$\times$ latency); zero-shot DR drops 76--99\% for calibrated models (7--8B; preliminary 70B in \S\ref{sec:70b-results}).
4	Triple validity analysis (\S\ref{sec:downstream-impact}) confirms 100\% schema compliance at $<$0.5\% recall cost.

1	% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)
2	
3	\section{Introduction}
4	\label{sec:introduction}
5	
6	Large language models are increasingly used for information extraction~\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity necessarily carries a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.
7	We call such outputs \emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.
8	Violations persist across the capability spectrum: 15.4\% of triples on SciERC~\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\% on DocRED~\cite{yao2019docred} under zero-shot decoding, and 0.5--2\% even after LoRA fine-tuning; rates correlate with schema restrictiveness (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology or model scale.
9	At deployment-grade quality (R-F1 40--46\%), even 1--2\% residual violations compound across thousands of documents into systematic knowledge graph corruption, making reliable constraint enforcement necessary.
10	
11	Existing approaches leave a fundamental gap.
12	Schema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \emph{increase} by 5.8\,pp).
13	Grammar-constrained decoding~\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} ensures syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.
14	Post-hoc filtering improves precision but discards up to 48\% of predictions including true positives; at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\%), it becomes impractical.
15	Training-based approaches~\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} internalize schema knowledge but provide only soft guidance without runtime enforcement; no method provides both semantic awareness and hard constraint enforcement.
16	
17	We propose \textbf{OntoCoDec}, a framework that translates IE ontologies into composable layers of decoding-time con
